When we reach line 2217, cnlen must be positive and cn must be valid, so the cast to size_t is safe and the comparison with strlen should work correctly. In the UTF8 case, cn comes from ASN1_STRING_get0_data(tmp) and cnlen from ASN1_STRING_length(tmp). The problem is that strlen() at line 2217 assumes cn is null-terminated, but ASN1_STRING data isn't guaranteed to be. If the string lacks a null terminator, strlen() will read past the buffer boundary until finding one, causing undefined behavior. (tmp);\n  cn = (unsigned char *)CURL_UNCONST(ASN1_STRING_get0_data(tmp));\n}\n```\n\nFor non-UTF8 strings, it converts to UTF8 and gets a new buffer. Then at line 2217, it compares the reported length against strlen to detect embedded nulls. The issue is that strlen could read past the buffer if the string isn't null-terminated, which is possible with ASN1_STRING data. In the UTF8 case especially, if ASN1_STRING_get0_data returns a non-null-terminated buffer, calling strlen on it would cause a buffer over-read.\n\nThe real problem is that this check assumes null-termination to detect embedded nulls, but that's unsafe. A better approach would be to scan the first cnlen bytes for a null byte rather than relying on strlen, which could read arbitrarily far past the actual data.\n\nLooking at the code path, ASN1_STRING_to_UTF8 likely null-terminates its output, but ASN1_STRING_get0_data in the UTF8 branch just returns an internal pointer that may not be null-terminated. This makes the strlen call particularly dangerous in that case.\n\nThe proper fix is to use memchr to detect embedded nulls within the known length rather than relying on null-termination. Something like checking if memchr(cn, '\\0', cnlen) finds anything would safely identify problematic embedded nulls without reading past the buffer bounds. + cnlen) {\n    /* there's an embedded null */\n    failf(data, \"SSL: illegal cert name field\");\n    result = CURLE_PEER_FAILED_VERIFICATION;\n}\n```\n\nThe core issue is that strlen reads beyond the buffer if the string isn't null-terminated. Using memchr to explicitly check for embedded nulls within the declared length is safer—it won't read past cnlen bytes and properly detects any null characters that would indicate a malformed certificate field. if the string isn't null-terminated, strlen will read past the buffer boundary. Using memchr instead provides a safer approach:\n\n```c\nif(memchr(cn, '\\0', (size_t)cnlen) != NULL) {\n  /* there was an embedded zero in the string, this\n     cannot match and we return failure! */\n  failf(data, \"SSL: illegal cert name field\");\n  result = CURLE_PEER_FAILED_VERIFICATION;\n}\n```\n\nThis checks for embedded nulls within the specified length without reading beyond cnlen bytes. In the non-UTF8 path, ASN1_STRING_to_UTF8 allocates a new buffer and returns its length, but null-termination isn't assured there either.
